Time-varying.\n    36→\n    37→### Decoder Outputs\n    38→- Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session. Time-varying.\n    39→\n    40→## Target Data Format\n    41→\n    42→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n    43→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    44→\n    45→```python\n    46→data = {\n    47→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    48→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    49→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    50→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    51→            ...\n    52→        ],\n    53→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    54→            ...\n    55→        ],\n    56→        ...\n    57→    ],\n    58→\n    59→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    60→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    61→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    62→            ...\n    63→        ],\n    64→        ...\n    65→    ],\n    66→\n    67→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    68→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    69→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    70→            ...\n    71→        ],\n    72→        ...\n    73→    ],\n    74→\n    75→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    76→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    77→\n    78→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    79→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    80→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    81→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    82→\n    83→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    84→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    85→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    86→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    87→        ...\n    88→    ],\n    89→\n    90→    'metadata': {\n    91→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n    92→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n    93→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n    94→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    95→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    96→        ...\n    97→    }\n    98→}\n    99→```\n   100→\n   101→### Key Format Requirements\n   102→\n   103→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   104→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   105→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   106→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   107→\n   108→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   109→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   110→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   111→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   112→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   113→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   114→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   115→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   116→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   117→\n   118→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   119→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   120→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   121→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   122→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   123→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   124→\n   125→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   126→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   127→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   128→\n   129→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   130→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   131→\n   132→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   133→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   134→\n   135→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   136→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   137→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   138→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   139→\n   140→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   141→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   142→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   143→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   144→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   145→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   146→\n   147→## Python environment\n   148→\n   149→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   150→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   151→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   152→\n   153→## Decoder Reference\n   154→\n   155→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   156→- Optional arguments:\n   157→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   158→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   159→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   160→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   161→\n   162→### Purpose\n   163→\n   164→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   165→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   166→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   167→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   168→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   169→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   170→\n   171→### Consistency requirements\n   172→\n   173→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   174→- Loading of data\n   175→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   176→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   177→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   178→\n   179→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   180→\n   181→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   182→\n   183→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder.\n   184→\n   185→Carefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n   186→\n   187→You will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n   188→\n   189→---\n   190→\n   191→## Conversion Workflow\n   192→\n   193→**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Each step has explicit completion criteria. Do not proceed to the next step until the current step is complete. Use the exact step names below when documenting progress in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   194→\n   195→**STATUS TRACKING**: At the start of each step, update its status to `IN PROGRESS` in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. When complete, update to `COMPLETE`. This helps you and the user track progress.\n   196→\n   197→---\n   198→\n   199→### Step 0: Setup and Initialization\n   200→\n   201→**Goal**: Set up the working environment and create the notes file.\n   202→\n   203→**Actions**:\n   204→1. **FIRST**: Create `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` with the template structure shown at the end of this document\n   205→2. Verify you can run `python3` and import key packages (numpy, torch)\n   206→3. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\n   207→\n   208→**Done when**: \n   209→- `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\n   210→- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\n   211→- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\n   212→\n   213→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la /app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\n   214→\n   215→---\n   216→\n   217→### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\n   218→\n   219→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `/app/code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   220→\n   221→**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\n   222→\n   223→**Actions**:\n   224→- Look for documentation or README files in the `/app/code` directory\n   225→- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\n   226→- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\n   227→- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \n   228→- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \n   229→- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\n   230→\n   231→**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 1\".\n   232→\n   233→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   234→\n   235→---\n   236→\n   237→### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n   238→\n   239→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `/app/data` directory. `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   341→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   342→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   343→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   344→- Write efficient code:\n   345→  - Vectorize loops\n   346→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   347→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   348→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   349→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   350→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   351→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   352→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   353→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   354→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   355→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   356→\n   357→**Done when**: `/app/convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   358→\n   359→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `/app/convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   360→\n   361→---\n   362→\n   363→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   364→\n   365→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   366→\n   367→**Actions**:\n   368→1. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   369→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   370→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   371→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   372→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   373→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   374→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   375→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   376→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   377→3. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   378→4. Analyze the output file `/app/verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   379→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   380→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   381→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   382→\n   383→**Done when**: \n   384→- `/app/sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   385→- `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   386→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   387→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   388→\n   389→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `/app/sample_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   390→\n   391→---\n   392→\n   393→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   394→\n   395→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   396→\n   397→**Actions**:\n   398→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   421→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   422→3. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_full_out.txt`\n   423→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   424→5. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_full_out.txt`. \n   425→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   426→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   427→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `/app/verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   428→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   429→\n   430→**Done when**:\n   431→- `/app/converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   432→- `/app/verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   433→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   434→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   435→\n   436→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `/app/converted_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   437→\n   438→---\n   439→\n   440→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   441→\n   442→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   443→\n   444→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   445→Read through `/app/verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   446→\n   447→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   448→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   449→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   450→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   451→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   452→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   453→- Document your sanity checks to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   454→\n   455→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   456→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   457→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   458→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   459→- Fix any mismatches. \n   460→- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   461→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   462→\n   463→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   464→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \n   465→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   466→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   467→- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   468→\n   469→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   470→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   471→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   472→\n   473→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   474→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   475→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   476→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   477→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   478→\n   479→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   480→\n   481→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   482→\n   483→---\n   484→\n   485→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   486→\n   487→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   488→\n   489→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   490→\n   491→**Actions**:\n   492→1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   493→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   494→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   495→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n   496→\n   497→**Done when**: \n   498→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   499→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   500→\n   501→---\n   502→\n   503→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   504→\n   505→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   506→\n   507→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   508→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   509→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   510→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   511→\n   512→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n   513→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n   514→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n   515→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   516→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   517→\n   518→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   519→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   520→\n   521→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   522→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   523→2. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING WHEN APPLICABLE**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference paper and code, except where the decoder input and output specifications or training a neural decoder require otherwise. You will be assessed on the statistics and decoder accuracy of your converted data, and on whether an expert would consider each of your decisions well justified.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository when applicable. This requires examining the provided paper, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your work in two ways. First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`.\n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\nDecode information regarding animal motion from the neural activities recorded from mouse barrel cortex. Split sessions into 60-second trials.\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time elapsed from the beginning of the session in seconds. Time-varying.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session. Time-varying.\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder.\n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time* derived from?\n   154→\n   155→i. Time is not derived from any raw data variable. Bins are 10 frames at 30 Hz, so the step is 1/3 s and a 30-minute session spans [0.0, 1799.67]. The value is the left edge of each bin and runs continuously across trials within a session. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Motion energy*?\n   186→\n   187→i. Three processing steps: (1) dropped frames are detected via interframe intervals >0.04 s and interpolated by averaging neighboring values, (2) the trace is averaged into 10-frame bins along with the neural data, (3) the binned signal is discretized into 5 percentile-based bins whose edges are computed **within each session**. \n   188→\n   189→ii.\n   190→```python\n   191→if me.shape[0] < expected_len:\n   192→    drop_indices = np.where(dt * 1000 > 0.04)[0]\n   193→    for offset, idx in enumerate(drop_indices):\n   194→        insert_pos = idx + 1 + offset\n   195→        interp_val = (me[insert_pos - 1] + me[insert_pos]) / 2.0\n   196→        me = np.insert(me, insert_pos, interp_val)\n   197→...\n   198→me = bin_frames(me)\n   199→...\n   200→percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_levels + 1)\n   201→for me in all_me_flat:\n   202→    bin_edges = np.percentile(me, percentiles)   # session-local edges\n   203→    output = np.digitize(me, bin_edges[1:-1])    # levels 0 .. n_levels-1\n   204→```\n   205→\n   206→iii. However, occasional video frames are dropped, making the motion energy array shorter than the neural data. Linear interpolation fills these gaps so that both streams can be indexed identically.\n   231→\n   232→## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n   233→\n   234→i. frames per bin (paper: \"averaging using a bin size of 10 frames\")\nFRAME_RATE = 30  # Hz\nNEUCOEFF = 0.7  # neuropil coefficient (Suite2p default)\nTRIAL_DURATION_SEC = 60  # seconds per trial\nN_ME_BINS = 5  # number of motion energy percentile bins\nBINNED_RATE = FRAME_RATE / BIN_SIZE  # 3 Hz\nTIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL = int(TRIAL_DURATION_SEC * BINNED_RATE)  # 180\n\n\ndef get_subjects_and_sessions(data_dir):\n    \"\"\"Get sorted list of subjects and their sessions.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted([d for d in os.listdir(data_dir)\n                       if os.path.isdir(os.path.join(data_dir, d)) and d.startswith('jm')])\n    all_sessions = {}\n    for subj in subjects:\n        subj_dir = os.path.join(data_dir, subj)\n        sessions = sorted([d for d in os.listdir(subj_dir)\n                          if os.path.isdir(os.path.join(subj_dir, d))])\n        all_sessions[subj] = sessions\n    return subjects, all_sessions\n\n\ndef load_session_data(data_dir, subject, session):\n    \"\"\"Load all data for a single session.\"\"\"\n    sess_dir = os.path.join(data_dir, subject, session)\n    s2p_dir = os.path.join(sess_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0')\n    move_dir = os.path.join(sess_dir, 'move_deve')\n\n    F = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'F.npy'))  # (n_neurons, n_frames)\n    Fneu = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'Fneu.npy'))  # (n_neurons, n_frames)\n    ops = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\n    me = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'motion_energy_glob.npy'))  # (n_cam_frames,)\n    tstamps = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'tstamps.npy'))  # (n_cam_frames,)\n\n    return F, Fneu, ops, me, tstamps\n\n\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu, ops):\n    \"\"\"Compute dF/F using Suite2p's baseline correction.\n    \n    Following the paper: \"We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F\n    (using the default Suite2p parameters)\"\n    \n    Steps:\n    1. Neuropil subtraction: Fc = F - 0.7 * Fneu\n    2. Baseline correction using maximin method (Suite2p default)\n    \"\"\"\n    # Step 1: Neuropil subtraction\n    Fc = F.copy() - NEUCOEFF * Fneu\n    \n    # Step 2: Baseline correction using Suite2p's preprocess function\n    # This applies the maximin baseline estimation and subtraction\n    import torch\n    device = torch.device('cuda' if torch.cuda.is_available() else 'cpu')\n    \n    dff = preprocess(\n        F=Fc.copy(),\n        baseline=ops.get('baseline', 'maximin'),\n        win_baseline=ops.get('win_baseline', 60.0),\n        sig_baseline=ops.get('sig_baseline', 10.0),\n        fs=ops.get('fs', 30.0),\n        prctile_baseline=ops.get('prctile_baseline', 8.0),\n        device=device\n    )\n    \n    return dff\n\n\ndef align_motion_energy(me, tstamps, n_neural_frames):\n    \"\"\"Align motion energy to neural frames, handling missing camera frames.\n    \n    When there are missing camera frames (me has fewer frames than neural),\n    we interpolate the motion energy to match the neural frame count.\n    \n    The tstamps array tells us which camera frames correspond to which timepoints.\n    We use the frame indices implied by tstamps to interpolate.\n    \"\"\"\n    n_cam_frames = len(me)\n    \n    if n_cam_frames == n_neural_frames:\n        # No missing frames\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    \n    # Missing frames case: interpolate motion energy to neural frame count\n    # Camera timestamps tell us when each camera frame was acquired\n    # Neural frames are at regular intervals (1/30 Hz)\n    # We need to map camera frames to neural frame indices\n    \n    # Create neural frame timestamps (regular spacing)\n    neural_times = np.arange(n_neural_frames) / FRAME_RATE  # in seconds\n    \n    # Camera timestamps are in kiloseconds, convert to seconds\n    cam_times = tstamps * 1000.0  # convert to seconds\n    \n    # Interpolate motion energy to neural frame times\n    me_aligned = np.interp(neural_times, cam_times, me.astype(np.float64))\n    \n    return me_aligned\n\n\ndef bin_data(data, bin_size, axis=-1):\n    \"\"\"Average data in non-overlapping bins along specified axis.\n    \n    Following the paper: \"slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces\n    by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\"\n    \"\"\"\n    if axis == -1:\n        axis = data.ndim - 1\n    \n    n = data.shape[axis]\n    n_bins = n // bin_size\n    # Truncate to multiple of bin_size\n    slices = [slice(None)] * data.ndim\n    slices[axis] = slice(0, n_bins * bin_size)\n    data_trunc = data[tuple(slices)]\n    \n    # Reshape and average\n    new_shape = list(data_trunc.shape)\n    new_shape[axis] = n_bins\n    new_shape.insert(axis + 1, bin_size)\n    data_reshaped = data_trunc.reshape(new_shape)\n    data_binned = data_reshaped.mean(axis=axis + 1)\n    \n    return data_binned\n\n\ndef split_into_trials(data_2d, timepoints_per_trial):\n    \"\"\"Split a 2D array (n_features x n_timepoints) into trials.\n    \n    Returns list of arrays, each (n_features x timepoints_per_trial).\n    Drops incomplete last trial.\n    \"\"\"\n    n_timepoints = data_2d.shape[-1]\n    n_trials = n_timepoints // timepoints_per_trial\n    \n    trials = []\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        start = t * timepoints_per_trial\n        end = start + timepoints_per_trial\n        if data_2d.ndim == 2:\n            trials.append(data_2d[:, start:end].copy())\n        else:  # 1D\n            trials.append(data_2d[start:end].copy())\n    \n    return trials\n\n\ndef discretize_motion_energy(me_binned_trials, n_bins=5):\n    \"\"\"Discretize motion energy into equal-percentile bins per session.\n    \n    Computes percentile boundaries from ALL trials in the session,\n    then applies to each trial.\n    \n    Returns discretized trials and bin edge info.\n    \"\"\"\n    # Concatenate all motion energy values from all trials in this session\n    all_me = np.concatenate(me_binned_trials)\n    \n    # Compute percentile boundaries for equal-frequency bins\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(all_me, percentiles)\n    \n    # Make bin edges unique to handle ties\n    # Use np.digitize with right=False to get bin indices 1..n_bins\n    # Then subtract 1 to get 0-indexed\n    discretized_trials = []\n    for me_trial in me_binned_trials:\n        # np.digitize returns indices such that bin_edges[i-1] <= x < bin_edges[i]\n        bin_indices = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1], right=False)\n        # Clip to valid range [0, n_bins-1]\n        bin_indices = np.clip(bin_indices, 0, n_bins - 1)\n        discretized_trials.append(bin_indices.astype(np.int64))\n    \n    return discretized_trials, bin_edges\n\n\ndef process_session(data_dir, subject, session, show_processing=False, session_idx=0):\n    \"\"\"Process a single session and return trial data.\"\"\"\n    t0 = time.time()\n    \n    # Load data\n    F, Fneu, ops, me, tstamps = load_session_data(data_dir, subject, session)\n    n_neurons, n_neural_frames = F.shape\n    n_cam_frames = len(me)\n    t_load = time.time() - t0\n    \n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Loaded: {n_neurons} neurons, {n_neural_frames} neural frames, \"\n          f\"{n_cam_frames} camera frames (load: {t_load:.2f}s)\")\n    \n    # Compute dF/F\n    t1 = time.time()\n    dff = compute_dff(F, Fneu, ops)\n    t_dff = time.time() - t1\n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] dF/F computed ({t_dff:.2f}s), range: [{dff.min():.2f}, {dff.max():.2f}]\")\n    \n    # Align motion energy to neural frames\n    me_aligned = align_motion_energy(me, tstamps, n_neural_frames)\n    \n    if n_cam_frames != n_neural_frames:\n        print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Motion energy interpolated: {n_cam_frames} -> {n_neural_frames} frames\")\n    \n    # Bin data (10 frames per bin)\n    t2 = time.time()\n    dff_binned = bin_data(dff, BIN_SIZE, axis=1)  # (n_neurons, n_bins)\n    me_binned = bin_data(me_aligned.reshape(1, -1), BIN_SIZE, axis=1).flatten()  # (n_bins,)\n    t_bin = time.time() - t2\n    \n    n_binned = dff_binned.shape[1]\n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Binned: {n_binned} timepoints ({t_bin:.2f}s)\")\n    \n    # Split into 60-second trials\n    neural_trials = split_into_trials(dff_binned, TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL)\n    me_trials = split_into_trials(me_binned, TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL)\n    n_trials = len(neural_trials)\n    \n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Split into {n_trials} trials of {TRIAL_DURATION_SEC}s \"\n          f\"({TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL} timepoints each)\")\n    \n    # Discretize motion energy (per session)\n    me_discrete_trials, bin_edges = discretize_motion_energy(me_trials, N_ME_BINS)\n    \n    # Create time input for each trial (time elapsed from session start in seconds)\n    input_trials = []\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        # Time from beginning of session\n        trial_start_sec = t * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC\n        time_in_trial = trial_start_sec + np.arange(TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL) / BINNED_RATE\n        input_trials.append(time_in_trial.astype(np.float32).reshape(1, -1))  # (1, n_timepoints)\n    \n    # Format output trials as (1, n_timepoints)\n    output_trials = [me.astype(np.int64).reshape(1, -1) for me in me_discrete_trials]\n    \n    # Convert neural to float32\n    neural_trials = [n.astype(np.float32) for n in neural_trials]\n    \n    t_total = time.time() - t0\n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Total processing time: {t_total:.2f}s\")\n    \n    # Plotting for --show-processing\n    if show_processing:\n        plot_processing(subject, session, session_idx,\n                       F, Fneu, dff, me, me_aligned, me_binned,\n                       dff_binned, neural_trials, me_trials, me_discrete_trials,\n                       input_trials, bin_edges)\n    \n    return neural_trials, input_trials, output_trials, n_neurons, bin_edges\n\n\ndef plot_processing(subject, session, session_idx,\n                   F, Fneu, dff, me_raw, me_aligned, me_binned,\n                   dff_binned, neural_trials, me_trials, me_discrete_trials,\n                   input_trials, bin_edges):\n    \"\"\"Plot processing steps for visual verification.\"\"\"\n    import matplotlib\n    matplotlib.use('Agg')\n    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt\n    \n    fig, axes = plt.subplots(6, 2, figsize=(20, 24))\n    fig.suptitle(f'Processing: {subject}/{session}', fontsize=16)\n    \n    # Sample neuron for plotting\n    nrn = 0\n    \n    # Row 0: Raw F and Fneu for sample neuron\n    ax = axes[0, 0]\n    ax.plot(F[nrn, :1000], label='F', alpha=0.7)\n    ax.plot(Fneu[nrn, :1000], label='Fneu', alpha=0.7)\n    ax.set_title(f'Raw fluorescence (neuron {nrn}, first 1000 frames)')\n    ax.legend()\n    ax.set_xlabel('Frame')\n    \n    # Row 0: dF/F for sample neuron\n    ax = axes[0, 1]\n    ax.plot(dff[nrn, :1000])\n    ax.set_title(f'dF/F (neuron {nrn}, first 1000 frames)')\n    ax.set_xlabel('Frame')\n    \n    # Row 1: Motion energy raw vs aligned\n    ax = axes[1, 0]\n    ax.plot(me_raw[:1000], label='Raw ME', alpha=0.7)\n    ax.plot(me_aligned[:1000], label='Aligned ME', alpha=0.7)\n    ax.set_title('Motion energy: raw vs aligned (first 1000 frames)')\n    ax.legend()\n    ax.set_xlabel('Frame')\n    \n    # Row 1: Binned motion energy\n    ax = axes[1, 1]\n    ax.plot(me_binned[:300])\n    ax.set_title('Binned motion energy (first 300 bins)')\n    ax.set_xlabel('Bin')\n    \n    # Row 2: dF/F binned - raster\n    ax = axes[2, 0]\n    n_show = min(50, dff_binned.shape[0])\n    ax.imshow(dff_binned[:n_show, :300], aspect='auto', cmap='gray_r', vmin=0, vmax=np.percentile(dff_binned[:n_show], 95))\n    ax.set_title(f'Binned dF/F raster (first {n_show} neurons, 300 bins)')\n    ax.set_xlabel('Bin')\n    ax.set_ylabel('Neuron')\n    \n    # Row 2: Histogram of motion energy with bin edges\n    ax = axes[2, 1]\n    ax.hist(np.concatenate(me_trials), bins=100, density=True, alpha=0.7)\n    for edge in bin_edges:\n        ax.axvline(edge, color='r', linestyle='--', alpha=0.5)\n    ax.set_title('Motion energy distribution with percentile bin edges')\n    ax.set_xlabel('Motion energy')\n    \n    # Row 3: Sample trial neural data\n    if len(neural_trials) > 0:\n        ax = axes[3, 0]\n        trial_idx = 0\n        ax.imshow(neural_trials[trial_idx][:n_show], aspect='auto', cmap='gray_r',\n                  vmin=0, vmax=np.percentile(neural_trials[trial_idx][:n_show], 95))\n        ax.set_title(f'Trial {trial_idx} neural data (first {n_show} neurons)')\n        ax.set_xlabel('Timepoint')\n        ax.set_ylabel('Neuron')\n    \n    # Both the neural and motion energy traces are averaged into non-overlapping bins of 10 consecutive frames, reducing 30 Hz to 3 Hz (333.33 ms time bin). Binning is applied before motion energy discretization.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_SIZE = 10  # frames per bin\nFRAME_RATE = 30  # Hz\n\ndef bin_data(data, bin_size, axis=-1):\n    n = data.shape[axis]\n    n_bins = n // bin_size\n    slices = [slice(None)] * data.ndim\n    slices[axis] = slice(0, n_bins * bin_size)\n    data_trunc = data[tuple(slices)]\n    new_shape = list(data_trunc.shape)\n    new_shape[axis] = n_bins\n    new_shape.insert(axis + 1, bin_size)\n    data_reshaped = data_trunc.reshape(new_shape)\n    data_binned = data_reshaped.mean(axis=axis + 1)\n    return data_binned\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES quote the paper: \"slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps.\"\n\n## 3-a. Three processing steps: (1) When camera frames are missing, the motion energy is interpolated to match neural frame count using `np.interp` with camera timestamps. (2) The trace is averaged into 10-frame bins. (3) The binned signal is discretized into 5 equal-percentile bins computed per session using `np.digitize`.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Step 1: Alignment/interpolation\ndef align_motion_energy(me, tstamps, n_neural_frames):\n    if n_cam_frames == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    neural_times = np.arange(n_neural_frames) / FRAME_RATE\n    cam_times = tstamps * 1000.0\n    me_aligned = np.interp(neural_times, cam_times, me.astype(np.float64))\n    return me_aligned\n\n# Step 2: Binning\nme_binned = bin_data(me_aligned.reshape(1, -1), BIN_SIZE, axis=1).flatten()\n\n# Step 3: Discretization\ndef discretize_motion_energy(me_binned_trials, n_bins=5):\n    all_me = np.concatenate(me_binned_trials)\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(all_me, percentiles)\n    for me_trial in me_binned_trials:\n        bin_indices = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1], right=False)\n        bin_indices = np.clip(bin_indices, 0, n_bins - 1)\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES explain that interpolation handles dropped camera frames, binning matches the paper's specification, and per-session percentile bins follow the task specification.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Motion energy* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Motion energy is discretized into 5 equal-percentile bins per session. Percentile boundaries are computed from all trials within a session using `np.linspace(0, 100, 6)` to get quintile edges, then `np.digitize` maps values to bins 0-4. Values are clipped to the valid range.\n\nii.\n```python\npercentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)\nbin_edges = np.percentile(all_me, percentiles)\nbin_indices = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1], right=False)\nbin_indices = np.clip(bin_indices, 0, n_bins - 1)\n```\n\niii. The task specification requires \"discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session.\" The AI computes bin edges from all trials of a session combined, then applies to each trial.\n\n## 4-d. When the camera frame count differs from the neural frame count (due to dropped frames), the AI uses `np.interp` with camera timestamps (`tstamps.npy`) to interpolate the motion energy to match the neural frame count. When frame counts match, no interpolation is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef align_motion_energy(me, tstamps, n_neural_frames):\n    n_cam_frames = len(me)\n    if n_cam_frames == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    neural_times = np.arange(n_neural_frames) / FRAME_RATE\n    cam_times = tstamps * 1000.0  # kiloseconds to seconds\n    me_aligned = np.interp(neural_times, cam_times, me.astype(np.float64))\n    return me_aligned\n```\n\niii. The AI uses continuous interpolation via `np.interp` with timestamp information, whereas the reference solution uses `interframe_int.npy` to detect dropped frames at specific positions and inserts interpolated values there. Both achieve the same goal of aligning motion energy to neural data.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. This is not a significant repetition but is unnecessary duplication.\n\nii.\n```python\nFc = F.copy() - NEUCOEFF * Fneu\ndff = preprocess(F=Fc.copy(), ...)\n```\n\niii. Parameters are read from the `ops.npy` file with fallback defaults matching Suite2p defaults.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu, ops):\n    Fc = F.copy() - NEUCOEFF * Fneu\n    dff = preprocess(\n        F=Fc.copy(),\n        baseline=ops.get('baseline', 'maximin'),\n        win_baseline=ops.get('win_baseline', 60.0),\n        sig_baseline=ops.get('sig_baseline', 10.0),\n        fs=ops.get('fs', 30.0),\n        prctile_baseline=ops.get('prctile_baseline', 8.0),\n        device=device\n    )\n    return dff\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES state: \"We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters)\". Parameters are read from ops.npy rather than hardcoded.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No additional quality filtering is applied. All neurons in the suite2p `F.npy` output are included. The CONVERSION_NOTES document that all cells in the data already have `iscell = 1.0` because Track2p outputs only tracked cells.\n\nii. N/A (no filtering code)\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES explain: \"All cells in the data are tracked cells (iscell all = 1.0)\" and \"Track2p filters cells using iscell_thr (probability threshold from Suite2p classifier).\"\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Trials are aligned to session start. Since trials are contiguous 60-second segments of the continuous recording, no event-based alignment is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\n'temporal_alignment_event': 'session_start',\n'off_start': 0.0,\n'off_end': float(TRIAL_DURATION_SEC),\n```\n\niii. There is no stimulus event to align to. The recording is continuous.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. Both the neural and motion energy traces are averaged into non-overlapping bins of 10 consecutive frames, reducing 30 Hz to 3 Hz (333.33 ms time bin). Binning is applied before motion energy discretization.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_SIZE = 10  # frames per bin\nFRAME_RATE = 30  # Hz\n\ndef bin_data(data, bin_size, axis=-1):\n    n = data.shape[axis]\n    n_bins = n // bin_size\n    slices = [slice(None)] * data.ndim\n    slices[axis] = slice(0, n_bins * bin_size)\n    data_trunc = data[tuple(slices)]\n    new_shape = list(data_trunc.shape)\n    new_shape[axis] = n_bins\n    new_shape.insert(axis + 1, bin_size)\n    data_reshaped = data_trunc.reshape(new_shape)\n    data_binned = data_reshaped.mean(axis=axis + 1)\n    return data_binned\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES quote the paper: \"slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps.\"\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time from start of experiment* derived from?\n\ni. Time is not derived from any raw data variable. It is computed from the trial index and the binned rate, giving seconds from the start of the session.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor t in range(n_trials):\n    trial_start_sec = t * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC\n    time_in_trial = trial_start_sec + np.arange(TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL) / BINNED_RATE\n    input_trials.append(time_in_trial.astype(np.float32).reshape(1, -1))\n```\n\niii. Since the frame rate is constant at 30 Hz and bins are of fixed size (10 frames), time can be computed purely from indices.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from start of experiment*?\n\ni. Time is computed as `trial_start_seconds + bin_index / binned_rate`. For trial 0, this gives `[0.0, 0.333, 0.667, ...]`. For trial 1, this gives `[60.0, 60.333, ...]`. The result is converted to float32.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrial_start_sec = t * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC\ntime_in_trial = trial_start_sec + np.arange(TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL) / BINNED_RATE\ninput_trials.append(time_in_trial.astype(np.float32).reshape(1, -1))\n```\n\niii. This produces the same values as computing from bin indices, since `bin_index / BINNED_RATE = bin_index * BIN_SIZE / FRAME_RATE`.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from start of experiment* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Time is directly computed from the same bin indices used for neural data, so alignment is inherent. Each time value corresponds to the left edge of the corresponding neural data time bin.\n\nii. (Same code as 3-b)\n\niii. Since both the neural data and time use the same indexing scheme, they are inherently aligned.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. Motion energy is derived from `motion_energy_glob.npy` in the `move_deve` subdirectory of each session. Camera timestamps from `tstamps.npy` are used for alignment when frames are missing.\n\nii.\n```python\nme = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'motion_energy_glob.npy'))\ntstamps = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'tstamps.npy'))\n```\n\niii. The motion energy file contains a pre-computed global motion energy signal from the behavioral video.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Motion energy*?\n\ni. Three processing steps: (1) When camera frames are missing, the motion energy is interpolated to match neural frame count using `np.interp` with camera timestamps. (2) The trace is averaged into 10-frame bins. (3) The binned signal is discretized into 5 equal-percentile bins computed per session using `np.digitize`.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Step 1: Alignment/interpolation\ndef align_motion_energy(me, tstamps, n_neural_frames):\n    if n_cam_frames == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    neural_times = np.arange(n_neural_frames) / FRAME_RATE\n    cam_times = tstamps * 1000.0\n    me_aligned = np.interp(neural_times, cam_times, me.astype(np.float64))\n    return me_aligned\n\n# Step 2: Binning\nme_binned = bin_data(me_aligned.reshape(1, -1), BIN_SIZE, axis=1).flatten()\n\n# Step 3: Discretization\ndef discretize_motion_energy(me_binned_trials, n_bins=5):\n    all_me = np.concatenate(me_binned_trials)\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(all_me, percentiles)\n    for me_trial in me_binned_trials:\n        bin_indices = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1], right=False)\n        bin_indices = np.clip(bin_indices, 0, n_bins - 1)\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES explain that interpolation handles dropped camera frames, binning matches the paper's specification, and per-session percentile bins follow the task specification.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Motion energy* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Motion energy is discretized into 5 equal-percentile bins per session. Percentile boundaries are computed from all trials within a session using `np.linspace(0, 100, 6)` to get quintile edges, then `np.digitize` maps values to bins 0-4. Values are clipped to the valid range.\n\nii.\n```python\npercentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)\nbin_edges = np.percentile(all_me, percentiles)\nbin_indices = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1], right=False)\nbin_indices = np.clip(bin_indices, 0, n_bins - 1)\n```\n\niii. The task specification requires \"discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session.\" The AI computes bin edges from all trials of a session combined, then applies to each trial.\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. When the camera frame count differs from the neural frame count (due to dropped frames), the AI uses `np.interp` with camera timestamps (`tstamps.npy`) to interpolate the motion energy to match the neural frame count. When frame counts match, no interpolation is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef align_motion_energy(me, tstamps, n_neural_frames):\n    n_cam_frames = len(me)\n    if n_cam_frames == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    neural_times = np.arange(n_neural_frames) / FRAME_RATE\n    cam_times = tstamps * 1000.0  # kiloseconds to seconds\n    me_aligned = np.interp(neural_times, cam_times, me.astype(np.float64))\n    return me_aligned\n```\n\niii. The AI uses continuous interpolation via `np.interp` with timestamp information, whereas the reference solution uses `interframe_int.npy` to detect dropped frames at specific positions and inserts interpolated values there. Both achieve the same goal of aligning motion energy to neural data.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Two types of data issues are handled: (1) Missing camera frames are detected by comparing camera frame count to neural frame count, and interpolated using timestamps. (2) Remainder bins at the end of a session that don't fill a complete 60-second trial are discarded. (3) Sessions with fewer than 2 trials are skipped.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Missing camera frames\nme_aligned = align_motion_energy(me, tstamps, n_neural_frames)\n\n# Incomplete last trial (in split_into_trials)\nn_trials = n_timepoints // timepoints_per_trial  # integer division drops remainder\n\n# Minimum trial check\nif len(neural_trials) < 2:\n    print(f\"  WARNING: Session {subj}/{sess} has only {len(neural_trials)} trials, skipping\")\n    continue\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES document that 8 sessions have missing camera frames (1-148 frames missing). The interpolation approach ensures both streams have matching lengths.\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is the Suite2p `preprocess` (baseline correction) function, which takes 0.07-0.47s per session depending on neuron count and GPU availability. Data loading is the second most expensive step (0.05-0.33s).\n\nii.\n```python\ndff = preprocess(\n    F=Fc.copy(),\n    baseline=ops.get('baseline', 'maximin'),\n    ...\n    device=device\n)\n```\n\niii. The conversion output shows per-session timing breakdowns. The baseline correction involves sliding window operations across all neurons for the full session length.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The `split_into_trials` function uses a Python loop to split data into trials, creating individual array copies. This could potentially be done with a single reshape operation (though the copies are intentional for memory independence).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef split_into_trials(data_2d, timepoints_per_trial):\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        start = t * timepoints_per_trial\n        end = start + timepoints_per_trial\n        if data_2d.ndim == 2:\n            trials.append(data_2d[:, start:end].copy())\n        else:\n            trials.append(data_2d[start:end].copy())\n    return trials\n```\n\niii. The loop is simple and fast relative to the baseline correction. The performance impact is negligible.\n\n## 6-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. The `F.copy()` is called once, and then `Fc.copy()` is passed to preprocess, making two copies of the fluorescence data. This is not a significant repetition but is unnecessary duplication.\n\nii.\n```python\nFc = F.copy() - NEUCOEFF * Fneu\ndff = preprocess(F=Fc.copy(), ...)\n```\n\niii. The double copy is a minor inefficiency. The expression `F.copy() - NEUCOEFF * Fneu` already creates a new array, so the initial `.copy()` is redundant.\n\n## 6-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\ni. The AI loads `ops.npy` and `tstamps.npy` for every session. The `ops.npy` values end up being the same as the hardcoded defaults, making the load unnecessary. The `tstamps.npy` is only needed for sessions with missing camera frames (~8 out of 41 sessions) but is loaded for all sessions.\n\nii.\n```python\nops = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\ntstamps = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'tstamps.npy'))\n```\n\niii. Loading `ops.npy` for every session adds a small overhead. In principle, reading parameters from ops is more robust than hardcoding, but in practice the values are the same across all sessions.\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}

A tail shorter than one\n        full bin is dropped, so the two streams stay the same length.\n    \"\"\"\n    n = (x.shape[-1] // k) * k\n    x = x[..., :n]\n    return x.reshape(*x.shape[:-1], n // k, k).mean(axis=-1)\n\n\ndef preprocess_calcium(session_path):\n    \"\"\"Load and preprocess calcium data for one session.   \"\"\"Load motion energy and interpolate dropped camera frames.\n    \"\"\"\n    me = np.load(os.path.join(session_path, 'move_deve', 'motion_energy_glob.npy'))\n    dt = np.load(os.path.join(session_path, 'move_deve', 'interframe_int.npy'))\n    print(f'    ME raw: {me.shape}  range [{me.min():.2f}, {me.max():.2f}]  (expected {expected_len})')\n\n    if me.shape[0] < expected_len:\n        drop_indices = np.where(dt * 1000 > 0.04)[0]\n        n_missing = expected_len - me.shape[0]\n        print(f'    missing {n_missing} frames, drops at indices: f'motion energy length {me.shape[0]} != expected {expected_len}'\n    )\n\n    print(f'    ME: range [{me.min():.4f}, {me.max():.4f}]  mean={me.mean():.4f}  std={me.std():.4f}')\n    return input)\n    all_bin_edges : list of 1-d arrays, the percentile boundaries used for each session\n    \"\"\"\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_levels + 1)\n\n    all_output = []\n    all_bin_edges = []\n    for me in all_me_flat:\n        bin_edges = np.percentile(me, percentiles)\n        output = np.digitize(me, bin_edges[1:-1])  # 0-indexed levels [0, n_levels-1]\n        all_output.append(output)\n        all_bin_edges.append(bin_edges)\n    return all_output, all_bin_edges\n\n\ndef main(out_path, base_path, sample=False):\n    subjects = get_subjects(base_path)\n print(f'\\n[{subject}] {len(sessions)} sessions')\n\n        for session_path in sessions:\n            session_name = os.path.basename(session_path)\n            print(f'  --- {session_name}                  f'({remainder/FS:.1f}s) that do not fill a full trial')\n\n            neural_trials = []\n            inp_trials = []\n            output_trials = []\n            for ti in np.arange(trial_frames)) * BIN_FRAMES / FS).astype(np.float32)\n\n                neural_trials.append(Fc[:, s:e])                  # (n_neurons, trial_frames)\n                inp_trials.append(t[np.newaxis, :])               # (1, trial_frames)\n                output_trials.append(out[np.newaxis, s:e])        # (1, trial_frames)\n\n {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_4","type":"command_execution","command":"/bin/bash -lc \"sed -n '1,320p' /app/convert_data.py\"","aggregated_output":"#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"Convert Track2p longitudinal calcium imaging data to decoder-compatible format.\n\nUsage:\n    python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile> [--full|--sample] [--show-processing]\n\"\"\"\n\nimport os\nimport sys\nimport time\nimport argparse\nimport pickle\nimport numpy as np\nimport warnings\n\n# Suppress warnings during suite2p import\nwith warnings.catch_warnings():\n    warnings.simplefilter(\"ignore\")\n    from suite2p.extraction.dcnv import preprocess\n\nDATA_DIR = '/app/data'\nBIN_SIZE = 10  # frames per bin (paper: \"averaging using a bin size of 10 frames\")\nFRAME_RATE = 30  # Hz\nNEUCOEFF = 0.7  # neuropil coefficient (Suite2p default)\nTRIAL_DURATION_SEC = 60  # seconds per trial\nN_ME_BINS = 5  # number of motion energy percentile bins\nBINNED_RATE = FRAME_RATE / BIN_SIZE  # 3 Hz\nTIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL = int(TRIAL_DURATION_SEC * BINNED_RATE)  # 180\n\n\ndef get_subjects_and_sessions(data_dir):\n    \"\"\"Get sorted list of subjects and their sessions.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted([d for d in os.listdir(data_dir)\n                       if os.path.isdir(os.path.join(data_dir, d)) and d.startswith('jm')])\n    all_sessions = {}\n    for subj in subjects:\n        subj_dir = os.path.join(data_dir, subj)\n        sessions = sorted([d for d in os.listdir(subj_dir)\n                          if os.path.isdir(os.path.join(subj_dir, d))])\n        all_sessions[subj] = sessions\n    return subjects, all_sessions\n\n\ndef load_session_data(data_dir, subject, session):\n    \"\"\"Load all data for a single session.\"\"\"\n    sess_dir = os.path.join(data_dir, subject, session)\n    s2p_dir = os.path.join(sess_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0')\n    move_dir = os.path.join(sess_dir, 'move_deve')\n\n    F = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'F.npy'))  # (n_neurons, n_frames)\n    Fneu = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'Fneu.npy'))  # (n_neurons, n_frames)\n    ops = np.load(os.path.join(s2p_dir, 'ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\n    me = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'motion_energy_glob.npy'))  # (n_cam_frames,)\n    tstamps = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'tstamps.npy'))  # (n_cam_frames,)\n\n    return F, Fneu, ops, me, tstamps\n\n\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu, ops):\n    \"\"\"Compute dF/F using Suite2p's baseline correction.\n    \n    Following the paper: \"We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F\n    (using the default Suite2p parameters)\"\n    \n    Steps:\n    1. Neuropil subtraction: Fc = F - 0.7 * Fneu\n    2. Baseline correction using maximin method (Suite2p default)\n    \"\"\"\n    # Step 1: Neuropil subtraction\n    Fc = F.copy() - NEUCOEFF * Fneu\n    \n    # Step 2: Baseline correction using Suite2p's preprocess function\n    # This applies the maximin baseline estimation and subtraction\n    import torch\n    device = torch.device('cuda' if torch.cuda.is_available() else 'cpu')\n    \n    dff = preprocess(\n        F=Fc.copy(),\n        baseline=ops.get('baseline', 'maximin'),\n        win_baseline=ops.get('win_baseline', 60.0),\n        sig_baseline=ops.get('sig_baseline', 10.0),\n        fs=ops.get('fs', 30.0),\n        prctile_baseline=ops.get('prctile_baseline', 8.0),\n        device=device\n    )\n    \n    return dff\n\n\ndef align_motion_energy(me, tstamps, n_neural_frames):\n    \"\"\"Align motion energy to neural frames, handling missing camera frames.\n    \n    When there are missing camera frames (me has fewer frames than neural),\n    we interpolate the motion energy to match the neural frame count.\n    \n    The tstamps array tells us which camera frames correspond to which timepoints.\n    We use the frame indices implied by tstamps to interpolate.\n    \"\"\"\n    n_cam_frames = len(me)\n    \n    if n_cam_frames == n_neural_frames:\n        # No missing frames\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    \n    # Missing frames case: interpolate motion energy to neural frame count\n    # Camera timestamps tell us when each camera frame was acquired\n    # Neural frames are at regular intervals (1/30 Hz)\n    # We need to map camera frames to neural frame indices\n    \n    # Create neural frame timestamps (regular spacing)\n    neural_times = np.arange(n_neural_frames) / FRAME_RATE  # in seconds\n    \n    # Camera timestamps are in kiloseconds, convert to seconds\n    cam_times = tstamps * 1000.0  # convert to seconds\n    \n    # Interpolate motion energy to neural frame times\n    me_aligned = np.interp(neural_times, cam_times, me.astype(np.float64))\n    \n    return me_aligned\n\n\ndef bin_data(data, bin_size, axis=-1):\n    \"\"\"Average data in non-overlapping bins along specified axis.\n    \n    Following the paper: \"slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces\n    by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\"\n    \"\"\"\n    if axis == -1:\n        axis = data.ndim - 1\n    \n    n = data.shape[axis]\n    n_bins = n // bin_size\n    # Truncate to multiple of bin_size\n    slices = [slice(None)] * data.ndim\n    slices[axis] = slice(0, n_bins * bin_size)\n    data_trunc = data[tuple(slices)]\n    \n    # Reshape and average\n    new_shape = list(data_trunc.shape)\n    new_shape[axis] = n_bins\n    new_shape.insert(axis + 1, bin_size)\n    data_reshaped = data_trunc.reshape(new_shape)\n    data_binned = data_reshaped.mean(axis=axis + 1)\n    \n    return data_binned\n\n\ndef split_into_trials(data_2d, timepoints_per_trial):\n    \"\"\"Split a 2D array (n_features x n_timepoints) into trials.\n    \n    Returns list of arrays, each (n_features x timepoints_per_trial).\n    Drops incomplete last trial.\n    \"\"\"\n    n_timepoints = data_2d.shape[-1]\n    n_trials = n_timepoints // timepoints_per_trial\n    \n    trials = []\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        start = t * timepoints_per_trial\n        end = start + timepoints_per_trial\n        if data_2d.ndim == 2:\n            trials.append(data_2d[:, start:end].copy())\n        else:  # 1D\n            trials.append(data_2d[start:end].copy())\n    \n    return trials\n\n\ndef discretize_motion_energy(me_binned_trials, n_bins=5):\n    \"\"\"Discretize motion energy into equal-percentile bins per session.\n    \n    Computes percentile boundaries from ALL trials in the session,\n    then applies to each trial.\n    \n    Returns discretized trials and bin edge info.\n    \"\"\"\n    # Concatenate all motion energy values from all trials in this session\n    all_me = np.concatenate(me_binned_trials)\n    \n    # Compute percentile boundaries for equal-frequency bins\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(all_me, percentiles)\n    \n    # Make bin edges unique to handle ties\n    # Use np.digitize with right=False to get bin indices 1..n_bins\n    # Then subtract 1 to get 0-indexed\n    discretized_trials = []\n    for me_trial in me_binned_trials:\n        # np.digitize returns indices such that bin_edges[i-1] <= x < bin_edges[i]\n        bin_indices = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1], right=False)\n        # Clip to valid range [0, n_bins-1]\n        bin_indices = np.clip(bin_indices, 0, n_bins - 1)\n        discretized_trials.append(bin_indices.astype(np.int64))\n    \n    return discretized_trials, bin_edges\n\n\ndef process_session(data_dir, subject, session, show_processing=False, session_idx=0):\n    \"\"\"Process a single session and return trial data.\"\"\"\n    t0 = time.time()\n    \n    # Load data\n    F, Fneu, ops, me, tstamps = load_session_data(data_dir, subject, session)\n    n_neurons, n_neural_frames = F.shape\n    n_cam_frames = len(me)\n    t_load = time.time() - t0\n    \n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Loaded: {n_neurons} neurons, {n_neural_frames} neural frames, \"\n          f\"{n_cam_frames} camera frames (load: {t_load:.2f}s)\")\n    \n    # Compute dF/F\n    t1 = time.time()\n    dff = compute_dff(F, Fneu, ops)\n    t_dff = time.time() - t1\n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] dF/F computed ({t_dff:.2f}s), range: [{dff.min():.2f}, {dff.max():.2f}]\")\n    \n    # Align motion energy to neural frames\n    me_aligned = align_motion_energy(me, tstamps, n_neural_frames)\n    \n    if n_cam_frames != n_neural_frames:\n        print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Motion energy interpolated: {n_cam_frames} -> {n_neural_frames} frames\")\n    \n    # Bin data (10 frames per bin)\n    t2 = time.time()\n    dff_binned = bin_data(dff, BIN_SIZE, axis=1)  # (n_neurons, n_bins)\n    me_binned = bin_data(me_aligned.reshape(1, -1), BIN_SIZE, axis=1).flatten()  # (n_bins,)\n    t_bin = time.time() - t2\n    \n    n_binned = dff_binned.shape[1]\n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Binned: {n_binned} timepoints ({t_bin:.2f}s)\")\n    \n    # Split into 60-second trials\n    neural_trials = split_into_trials(dff_binned, TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL)\n    me_trials = split_into_trials(me_binned, TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL)\n    n_trials = len(neural_trials)\n    \n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Split into {n_trials} trials of {TRIAL_DURATION_SEC}s \"\n          f\"({TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL} timepoints each)\")\n    \n    # Discretize motion energy (per session)\n    me_discrete_trials, bin_edges = discretize_motion_energy(me_trials, N_ME_BINS)\n    \n    # Create time input for each trial (time elapsed from session start in seconds)\n    input_trials = []\n    for t in range(n_trials):\n        # Time from beginning of session\n        trial_start_sec = t * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC\n        time_in_trial = trial_start_sec + np.arange(TIMEPOINTS_PER_TRIAL) / BINNED_RATE\n        input_trials.append(time_in_trial.astype(np.float32).reshape(1, -1))  # (1, n_timepoints)\n    \n    # Format output trials as (1, n_timepoints)\n    output_trials = [me.astype(np.int64).reshape(1, -1) for me in me_discrete_trials]\n    \n    # Convert neural to float32\n    neural_trials = [n.astype(np.float32) for n in neural_trials]\n    \n    t_total = time.time() - t0\n    print(f\"  [{subject}/{session}] Total processing time: {t_total:.2f}s\")\n    \n    #
